- 2026-10-22T19:55 zzyyxx332211 https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50200180 :: Thanks! The percentage rules don't depend on the plan as it reads the 5-hour and weekly percentages Claude Code reports, so asking at 80% and refusing at 95% works the same on Pro or Max 20x. Same for the burn rule (20 points of the 5-hour window in 10 minutes). The ones that depend on the plan are the absolute numbers: 4 agents running at once, 12 agent starts per 10 minutes, 10M subagent tokens per 10 minutes, and the 500k context before it asks to compact. I tuned them on my own Max 5x, so on Pro they are probably too loose. You can change all of them with env vars (AGENT_GUARD_*), but presets per plan is a good idea, I'll look into it.

Unlike search, which shows what people are looking up, Reddit shows what they're talking about, so I feel it complements Google Trends as another interesting data source. It also has the benefit of being able to narrow things down to a single subreddit. I've normalized trends by unique posters per day instead of raw mentions. It's not perfect, but without it a single account (e.g. AutoModerator on one subreddit) can make it look like there's a trend when there isn't, especially for less common words. I built a few features I haven't seen elsewhere, and ended up using them a lot: Sentiment - the weekly net sentime

- 2026-10-25T01:21 ffseika0304 https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50227262 :: Operating a Go gateway in production (single machine, ~20B tokens/month across several upstreams), usage-reporting quirks are exactly where our pain concentrated too. What survived for us: - Treat every upstream try as an attempt with its own usage record, and let request cost be whatever the attempts sum to. Without attempt-level accounting, retries make your per-request numbers lie: you count the first attempt's tokens but pay for the retry.
- Vendor-reported usage is advisory, not gospel. Some providers emit usage only on the final SSE chunk, some want stream_options, some never report it for streams at all. The parser zoo is unavoidable, but the damage is contained if the rest of the sys

- 2026-10-24T21:12 Aardappel https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50225350 :: Certainly, this is a language where you get the most benefits if you are able to structure your data usage such that things of similar lifetimes sit together in arrays. For example, you can build up a tree of all variable sized nodes inside a single array. The language has various tools for this, like fixed arrays with capacity, or pools of either single elements or runs of elements.

This stops source address spoofing  dead . RFC 2827 (BCP 38) was published in May  2000 . There's no good excuse for end-user ISPs to not be doing this for pretty much all of their customers.       x *= 0xed5ad4bb;
      x ^= x >> 11;
      x = Math.imul(x,0xac4c1b51);
      x ^= x >> 15;
      x = Math.imul(x,0x31848bab);
      x ^= x >> 14;
      return x;
   }

   const makeRandom = seed => {
      let index=0;
      let random = (x=index) => {
        let pattern = intHash( (x + seed) & 0x7fffffff)
        let intValue = intHash( (x + pattern)  & 0x7fffffff);
        index=++x;
        return intValue / 0x7fffffff;
      }
      return random;

- 2026-10-22T19:55 zzyyxx332211 https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50200180 :: Thanks! - 2026-10-24T19:48 antonvs https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50224484 :: Unusual word combinations, basically. They’re word combinations that are rare in the training data, i.e. rare in normal language, but used more frequently by AI. They’re difficult for humans because if a sequence of words hasn’t previously been seen, you have to expend more effort to make sense of it. Something like “customer onboarding workflow automation" might qualify - it makes sense, but it’s not a commonly used phrase. AIs use a lot of these jargony sequences.

In 1854, Dr. John Snow famously put cholera death data on a map to help readers see that reported deaths in Soho, London were in the vicinity of the Broad Street pump, which was the source of the contagion, as well as to shift from an understanding of cholera as airborne to waterborne. That ended up eating 90GB after we let it loose on a Windows VM that we connected with a custom agent between codex and the VM. This is definitely a Codex thing and I've never seen this happen in Claude Code or Antigravity.

- 2026-10-25T14:26 latexr https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50231939 :: I don’t know why you’d want to remember the number, but this one seems easy if you know some memory techniques. > Remember that vuln 111-497 (as two chunks) Good first step. - 2026-10-25T12:11 abainbridge https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50230749 :: > What I understand from that is, byte code is portable while machine code isn't. This is an area I want to explore. It seems to me that it is less true than it used to be. x86_32 was the last significant architecture that made things hard. Now we can ignore that. All desktop or server class CPUs support 8, 16, 32 and 64-bit integers. And they all have at least 10 general purpose registers. They all have a sensible set of memory access modes. As a result, the same code generator works for them all. - 2026-10-25T12:11 abainbridge https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50230749 :: > What I understand from that is, byte code is portable while machine code isn't. This is an area I want to explore. It seems to me that it is less true than it used to be. x86_32 was the last significant architecture that made things hard. Now we can ignore that. All desktop or server class CPUs support 8, 16, 32 and 64-bit integers. And they all have at least 10 general purpose registers. They all have a sensible set of memory access modes. As a result, the same code generator works for them all. It seems natural to assume that something here was pre-determined.  i.e. There's was some kind of hidden information that we haven't found a way to measure yet. The way in which the coin-tosses were going to pan out was determined when the pair of particles were entangled. Quantum physics makes predictions that suggest this isn't so.  You can design experiments (e.g. A Bell inequality test) in which quantum physics predicts correlations t

- 2026-10-22T19:55 zzyyxx332211 https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50200180 :: Thanks! The percentage rules don't depend on the plan as it reads the 5-hour and weekly percentages Claude Code reports, so asking at 80% and refusing at 95% works the same on Pro or Max 20x. Same for the burn rule (20 points of the 5-hour window in 10 minutes). The ones that depend on the plan are the absolute numbers: 4 agents running at once, 12 agent starts per 10 minutes, 10M subagent tokens per 10 minutes, and the 500k context before it asks to compact. I tuned them on my own Max 5x, so on Pro they are probably too loose. You can change all of them with env vars (AGENT_GUARD_*), but presets per plan is a good idea, I'll look into it.

- 2026-09-26T02:24 aaroninsf https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49852597 :: I use plan mode, because my current project benefits from "pair programming."
